a communicating system is specified in terms of components,
each (
composite
) component contains a
choreography (or,
protocol
) and for each role
in the protocol a new implementation (component) is specified.
Intuitively speaking, each component comes with
an input/output interface allowed by the protocol,
so to keep track of data-flow dependencies.

Montesi and Yoshida 2013
develop a compositional
technique for choreographic
programming: the specification of partial choreographies
is allowed, i.e., the implementation of some of the
roles can be left unspecified.
These roles can be implemented by a different choreography
at a later time;
compatibility
is achieved through an additional typing
relation on choreographies,
which relies both on global and local types.
All the above work modifies the essence of
the protocol structure. Our theory differs
from it, first, because
our compatibility condition
(Equation
C
)
relies on projection,
instead of dual input/output matching.
Then, compositionality based on
hybrid types retains the
simplicity of traditional MPST
protocol structure:
the result of composition
is a well-formed global type
and no new semantics need to be developed.
Thanks to such semantics preservation and our novel compatibility,
our techniques are general enough
to be applied to traditional MPST
(§
3
and §
4
),
as well as to more expressive specifications (§
5.4
).

Modular Global Types through Nesting.

Global types are choreographic objects
and, hence, originally
(
Honda et al. 2008
)

intended as standalone entities.
Given their monolithic nature, techniques
for making them more flexible have been
proposed very early on; e.g.,
Demangeon and Honda 2012
describe a methodology for nesting global types,
via calls to a subprotocol from a parent protocol.

Tabareau et al. 2014
propose an alternative
approach to nesting protocols,
by extending the syntax of
global and local types with

aspects

(
Kiczales et al. 1997
)
.
Demangeon et al. 2015

also explore nesting techniques for global types,
and apply these to extend the Scribble
protocol description language

(
Honda et al. 2011
;
Neykova and Yoshida 2019
;
Yoshida et al. 2021
)

with interruptible interactions.
Our work does not establish a parent/offspring
relation, but it explores direct composition of
subprotocols treated as peers.

State of The Art of Direct Composition of MPST Protocols.

To the best of
our knowledge, only
Barbanera et al. 2021
and
Stolze et al. 2021

investigate the direct composition of protocols
for MPST, so that

the result of composition is a well-formed global type
.
Our development differs from this work, in primis, because of its
semantics preservation: in our theory, global types are exactly as in
traditional MPST theories (e.g.,
(
Deniélou and Yoshida 2013
)
);
thus, our compositionality is
immediately compatible with those
and benefits from their semantics results.
Furthermore, our theory
can compose
more than two
protocols
(missing in
(
Barbanera et al. 2021
)
) and captures the full
expressiveness of MPST, including
parallel composition
and recursion
(missing in
(
Stolze et al. 2021
)
).
Ultimately, our compatibility (Equation
C
)

overcomes the limitations of dual input/output matching
,
on which both
Barbanera et al. 2021
and
Stolze et al. 2021
rely:
our theory is general enough
to be applied to more sophisticated
MPST systems
(§
5.4
, Example
5.7
).
Below we expand on the significance of our contribution,
with respect to this related work, with detailed examples.

In
(
Barbanera et al. 2021
)
, the composition of

two
global types
is achieved with

gateways

(
Barbanera et al. 2018
;
Barbanera et al. 2019
;
Barbanera et al. 2020b
)
: two participants,
one for each subprotocol (global type), are selected as
forwarders (gateways) for communicating with the other;
if the subprotocols
are compatible, with respect to the
gateway choice, they can be composed
into a more general global type.
The central difference between our design and
(
Barbanera et al. 2021
)

is the “interface” through which subprotocols
communicate. Instead of gateways, hybrid types use local
constructs for inter-component interactions and,
instead of a dual input/output matching, they
rely on projection for compatibility.
Thus, we
can safely combine two or more protocols at once.
Concretely, we consider the
following example involving three subprotocols,
described
as global types with gateways.

\begin{array}[]{c}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G}{}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{h$\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{}_{2}}$}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{q}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{h$\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{}_{3}}$}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{q}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hskip 18.49988pt\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G}{}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{k$\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{}_{1}}$}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{r}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{s}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{r}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{r}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{k$\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{}_{3}}$}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\\
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G}{}_{3}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{t}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{u}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{u}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{l$\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{}_{1}}$}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{l$\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{}_{2}}$}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{u}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\end{array}

We identify
({{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{h$\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{}_{2}}$}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{k$\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{}_{1}}$}}}})
,
({{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{h$\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{}_{3}}$}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{l$\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{}_{1}}$}}}})
,
and
({{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{k$\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{}_{3}}$}}}},{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{l$\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{}_{2}}$}}}})
as gateways;
we choose the first one and compose.

\begin{array}[]{l}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G}{}_{1}}{}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{h$\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{}_{2}}$}}}}\leftrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{k$\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{}_{1}}$}}}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G}{}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{h$\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{}_{2}}$}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{h$\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{}_{2}}$}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{k$\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{}_{1}}$}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{k$\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{}_{1}}$}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{r}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{q}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{h$\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{}_{3}}$}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{q}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{s}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{r}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{r}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{k$\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{}_{3}}$}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}}\end{array}

The next steps of composition should happen between
h
3
and
l
1
,
and between
k
3
and
l
2
.
Applying again gateway-composition does not work:
after a first step (e.g., using
h
3
and
l
1
) we would
be left with a single global type, i.e.,
no two types left to compose
(e.g., using
k
3
and
l
2
). While we
could try simultaneous composition for more
than one pair of gateways at once, the simple
example in
(
Barbanera et al. 2021
)
, Section
11
, shows how this extension is
unsound and could lead to deadlocked systems.
Another naïve attempt to improve on gateway
composition is the following:
we force
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G}{}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G}{}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

to have the same participant for communicating with
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G}{}_{3}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

(relaxing the condition from
(
Barbanera et al. 2021
)
,
where participants in two composing global types must be distinct):

\begin{array}[]{c}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G}{}^{\prime}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{h}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{l}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{p}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hskip 18.49988pt\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G}{}^{\prime}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{k}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{q}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{q}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{l}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\\
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G}{}^{\prime}_{3}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{t}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{r}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{r}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{t}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\mathtt{end}}\end{array}

The communication is supposed to happen between
h
and
k
,
and between
l
and
t
. If we allow non-distinct participants
in distinct global types, however, it is not immediate
to achieve a well-defined gateways compositionality.
For example, the order in which we compose plays a key role:
while
(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G}{}_{2}}{}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{k}}}}\leftrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{h}}}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G}{}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})^{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{l}}}}\leftrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{t}}}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G}{}_{3}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

is well defined,

(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G}{}_{1}}{}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{h}}}}\leftrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{k}}}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G}{}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})^{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{l}}}}\leftrightarrow{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{t}}}}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G}{}_{3}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

is not. Also, associativity would not hold:
we cannot compose
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G}{}_{3}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
with any
of
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G}{}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G}{}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and then finish
composing with the remaining one.

We specify the system above in terms of hybrid types:
in place of gateways,
we use local constructs for inter-component
communication (highlighted),
and we give the type
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G^{\dagger}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
for compatibility.

\begin{array}[]{c}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\hbox{\pagecolor{lightyellow}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{p}}}}}!{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{r}}}}};}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{q}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\hbox{\pagecolor{papayawhip}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{u}}}}}?{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{q}}}}};}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hskip 18.49988pt\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\hbox{\pagecolor{lightyellow}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{p}}}}}?{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{r}}}}};}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{s}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{r}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\hbox{\pagecolor{lightyg}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{r}}}}}!{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{u}}}}};}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}}\\
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H_{3}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{t}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{u}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\hbox{\pagecolor{papayawhip}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{u}}}}}!{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{q}}}}};}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\hbox{\pagecolor{lightyg}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{r}}}}}?{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}{{u}}}}};}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hskip 18.49988pt\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G^{\dagger}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\hbox{\pagecolor{lightyellow}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{ ${{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{r}}}}:$}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\hbox{\pagecolor{papayawhip}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{ ${{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{u}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{q}}}}:$}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\hbox{\pagecolor{lightyg}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{ ${{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{r}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{u}}}}:$}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}}\end{array}

For all components, compatibility
C
holds
and our theory can compose the three protocols into a well-formed global type
(Corollary
4.10
), thus overcoming the
binary
limitation of gateways.

Stolze et al. 2021
, develop
a binary compatibility
relation that
partially improves on

(
Barbanera et al. 2021
)
: they can compose
more than two types,
but put severe restrictions on the syntax.
Global types in
(
Stolze et al. 2021
)

are inductive, but
with
no recursion and no parallel construct
,
thus making our work (and traditional MPST in general)
strictly more expressive.
The key role of parallel composition
for an inductive syntax
is discussed in
(
Glabbeek et al. 2021
)

(Example 13 and following paragraph)
and in §
5.1
.
Recursion is paramount for expressiveness in MPST

(
Honda et al. 2008
;
Honda et al. 2016
;
Coppo et al. 2015
;
Yoshida and Gheri 2020
)
:
it allows typing processes with loops
and it is
omnipresent in real-world protocols
(e.g., see §
5.2

and §
5.3
).
As for semantics,
recursion allows for infinite executions and is
a key element in the correspondence between local
types and communicating finite-state machines

(
Deniélou and Yoshida 2013
)
, on which
practical implementations
of the popular Scribble protocol language
are based

(
Honda et al. 2011
;
Yoshida et al. 2013
;
Hu and Yoshida 2016
;
Neykova and Yoshida 2019
;
Yoshida et al. 2021
)
.
Also, recursion (in combination with branching)
is among the most delicate aspects of MPST
(see, e.g., Observation 3 in
(
Glabbeek et al. 2021
)

or Definition
B.1

and the following well-formedness lemmas
in Appendix
B.1
).
E.g., the recursive types

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\mu\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{q}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell_{1}}}}.\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{p}}}}}!{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{r}}}}};}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\mu\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{p}}}}}?{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{r}}}}};}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{s}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{r}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell_{2}}}}.\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
,
are composable both with gateways
(
Barbanera et al. 2021
)

and with our theory
(
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G^{\dagger}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\mu\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{r}}}}:}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell}}}.\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

for compatibility), but they cannot be expressed in
(
Stolze et al. 2021
)
.

In summary, our theory allows composing
two or more

subprotocols into a well-formed global type, while
retaining
full expressiveness
of MPST, thus improving
on the state of the art
(
Barbanera et al. 2021
;
Stolze et al. 2021
)
.
Furthermore, previous work
focuses on dual input/output matching,
which makes it inapplicable to more expressive MPST systems,
where instead, to the best of our knowledge,
our novel compatibility
C
is the first notion
to succeed (see §
5.4
, Example
5.7
).
We observe that, as it happens
for the extension of
binary session types to multiparty session types,
when relying on projection instead of duality,
we commit to the specification of one more global
object (what we called
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G^{\dagger}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
), but, in return,
we obtain full multiparty compatibility.

7.
Future Work

The first envisioned application for
our compositionality theory
is its integration with
practical protocol design
languages, such as
Zooid
(
Castro-Perez et al. 2021
)
or Scribble

(
Honda et al. 2011
;
Yoshida et al. 2013
;
Yoshida et al. 2021
)
.
In what follows we
briefly describe
how this integration
can be realised.

In the past ten years,
Scribble has been employed as
the protocol language
of multiple toolchains,
supporting different
programming languages and
integrating a variety of
expressive features

(
Honda et al. 2011
;
Yoshida et al. 2013
;
Neykova and Yoshida 2019
;
Yoshida et al. 2021
;
Hu and Yoshida 2017
;
Scalas et al. 2017
;
Neykova et al. 2018
;
Castro et al. 2019
;
Miu et al. 2021
;
Zhou et al. 2020
;
Gheri et al. 2022
)
.
Independently of the
specific implementation,
the Scribble toolchain is generally
designed as follows.

(1)

The designer specifies the communication
protocol (a global type
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
) in Scribble.

(2)

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is projected onto local types

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{L}{}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\dots,\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{L}{}_{n}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
—or,
equivalently, their representation
as CFSMs
(
Deniélou and Yoshida 2013
)
.

(3)

From local types, APIs for
the distributed implementation
of all participants
are generated
(following the approach of
Hu and Yoshida 2016
).

Through API implementation,
the communication
for the multiparty
system is MPST certified
and does not get stuck
(semantic guarantees hold).
Thanks to its semantics-preserving
features and its backwards compatibility with
existing MPST systems,
our compositionality framework can be
integrated with Scribble toolchains,
with minimal effort. First,
we will extend
the Scribble protocol design language
to the syntax of hybrid types
(Definition
3.3
)—of which
global types are a particular case—with
constructs
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{p}}}}}!{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{q}}}}};}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{p}}}}}?{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{q}}}}};}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.
Then, we will implement the localiser
(Definition
3.8
) and extend
projection to Definition
3.6
;
here compatibility with previous
implementations is guaranteed by the
considerations in Remark
3.7
:
Definition
3.6
generalises
traditional MPST projection
(Definition
3.2
).
Finally, we will implement checks
for
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{isGlobal(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G^{\dagger}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5})}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and compatibility

C

(
\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G^{\dagger}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\upharpoonright}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{E}}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf{loc}}\;}}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{E}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
).
With these simple changes,
we will
endow Scribble
with compositionality, thus enabling
distributed protocol specification.
“Compositional Scribble”
will look as follows.

(1^{\text{c}})

Multiple designers give
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\dots,\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H_{N}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}

in a distributed fashion and a chief designer specifies

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G^{\dagger}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, as
hybrid types
.

(2^{\text{c}})

Internal checks are performed for

\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{isGlobal(\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5}{G^{\dagger}}\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0.5})}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and compatibility
C
.

(3^{\text{c}})

Each
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is projected onto local types

\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{L}{}^{i}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\dots,\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}{L}{}^{i}_{n_{i}}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
—or,
equivalently, their representation
as CFSMs
(
Deniélou and Yoshida 2013
)
.

(4^{\text{c}})

From local types, APIs for
the distributed implementation
of all participants
are generated.

We observe that, thanks to our theory, the
checks in
(2^{\text{c}})
are
enough to guarantee the existence of a well-formed global
type for the whole system,
without the need for explicitly building back such type
(see also Corollary
4.10

and Remark
4.12
).
Moreover, since projection is preserved
(Theorem
4.9
and Corollary
4.10
),
local types are the same as in traditional MPST:
not only do semantic guarantees still hold,
but so does the correspondence between local
types and CFSMs in
(
Deniélou and Yoshida 2013
)
.

With respect to traditional Scribble,
nothing changes for the user, apart from
the added functionality of distributed
protocol specification: the safe distributed
implementation of all participants
is still enabled, but, now, also protocols
can be specified in a distributed fashion,
in terms of their
components (as hybrid types
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\dots,\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H_{N}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
).

Beyond its integration with existing
protocol design languages, we
plan to build on this work in
different directions.
In Section
5.4
,
we have shown the potential
of our approach to compositionality, by adapting it
to an MPST formalism that extends
the traditional syntax of global types
to include the advanced features
of delegation and explicit connections.
Similarly, beyond our
core compositionality for
global types,
we envision
future applications
to the wide
variety of MPST systems
(e.g., featuring fault tolerance
(
Viering et al. 2021
)
,
timed specification
(
Bocchi et al. 2014
)
,
refinements
(
Zhou et al. 2020
)
,
cost awareness
(
Castro-Perez and Yoshida 2020
)
, or
exception handling
(
Lagaillardie et al. 2022
)
)
and, orthogonally, future extensions that add flexibility to compositionality
itself (e.g., by factoring in
renaming mechanisms for participants
(
Jacobs et al. 2022
)
).
Another promising
perspective is the application of our techniques beyond MPST, to other
protocol-design formalisms
based on projection, e.g.,

choreography automata

(
Barbanera et al. 2020a
;
Gheri et al. 2022
)
or

choreographic programming

(
Montesi 2013
)
.

8.
Conclusion

We have developed a
“compositionality-through-projection”
technique
that allows the
distributed specification
of MPST protocols,
in terms of
hybrid types

(Definition
3.3
).
Our work neatly
improves on the state of the art,
by allowing for composition
of
more than two protocols
,
while retaining the
full
expressiveness
of global types.
Our results
(Corollaries
4.10
and
5.1
)
guarantee correctness and make
our theory compatible
with existing MPST systems (
semantics preservation
). Our
novel compatibility
relation
(Equation
C
), based on
generalised projection
and localiser
(Definitions
3.6
and
3.8
),
overcomes the limitations of dual input/output matching
and it is general enough
to capture extensions beyond traditional MPST
(e.g., to delegation and explicit connections
§
5.4
).

Research and Innovation Programme,
grant agreement 101093006 (TaRDIS).

Appendix B
Definitions and Proofs

B.1.
Compositionality: Building Back a Subprotocol into a Global One

Definition B.1 (Guardedness for Hybrid Types).

We define
guardedness
as a predicate on hybrid types, with the following inductive rules:

\begin{array}[]{c}\dfrac{}{\texttt{\small guarded}\ \color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\quad\dfrac{}{\texttt{\small guarded}\ \color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}\quad\dfrac{\texttt{\small not\_pure\_rec}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\quad\texttt{\small guarded}\ \color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\texttt{\small guarded}\ (\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\mu\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}\quad\dfrac{\texttt{\small guarded}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\quad\texttt{\small guarded}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}{\texttt{\small guarded}\ (\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H_{1}}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}|\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H_{2}}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}\\[8.53581pt]
\dfrac{\forall i\in I.\texttt{\small guarded}\ {\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}{}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}{\texttt{\small guarded}\ (\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{p}}}}}!{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{q}}}}};\{{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell_{i}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{\texttt{\small S}}}_{i}}}).\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}\quad\dfrac{\forall i\in I.\texttt{\small guarded}\ {\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}{}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}{\texttt{\small guarded}\ (\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{p}}}}}?{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{q}}}}};\{{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell_{i}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{\texttt{\small S}}}_{i}}}).\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}\quad\dfrac{\forall i\in I.\texttt{\small guarded}\ {\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}{}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}{\texttt{\small guarded}\ (\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{p}}}}\to{{\color[rgb]{0.0039,0.4727,0.4336}\textbf{{q}}}}:\{{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5508}{\ell_{i}}}}({\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{{\color[rgb]{0.2813,0.2383,0.5469}{\texttt{\small S}}}_{i}}}).\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}{}_{i}\}_{i\in I}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}\end{array}\vskip 8.53581pt

where
\texttt{\small not\_pure\_rec}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
means that
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is different
from
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\mu\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{Y_{1}}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.\dots\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\mu\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{Y_{n}}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and also

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\neq\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.

The above definition is adapted from the Coq-mechanised MPST metatheory in
(
Castro-Perez et al. 2021
)
(Definitions A.2 and A.10 in the appendix of the extended version of the paper
(
Castro-Perez et al. 2021
)
).

The next six lemmas are preliminary results, adapted from classic
multiparty session type theories. They are
needed for the base cases of induction
in the build-back theorem (Theorem
B.17
). For all the
lemmas below, the proofs go by structural induction.

Lemma B.2 (Projection and Closed Types).

\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\upharpoonright}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{E}}
is closed iff
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is closed.

Lemma B.3 (Localiser and Closed Types).

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf{loc}}\;}}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is closed iff
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is closed.

Lemma B.4 (Projection onto Internal Participants).

If
{\textsf{ipart}}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\subseteq E
then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\upharpoonright}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{E}}=\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.

Lemma B.5 (Projection onto Disjoint Participants
(1)
).

If
{\textsf{ipart}}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\cap E^{\prime}=\emptyset
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is closed, then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\upharpoonright}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{E^{\prime}}}=\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.

Lemma B.6 (Projection onto Disjoint Participants
(2)
).

If
{\textsf{ipart}}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\cap E^{\prime}=\emptyset
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is
not
closed, then
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\upharpoonright}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{E^{\prime}}}=\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\mu Y_{1}.\dots.\mu Y_{n}.}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
(if such projection exists).

Lemma B.7 (Projection is Guarded).

If
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\upharpoonright}}_{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{E}}
is guarded (if it is defined and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
is guarded).

The following definitions and lemmas introduce objects and results
needed for the main algorithm and ultimately
for proving Theorem
B.17
.
They guarantee the good behaviour of our compositionality algorithm,
with respect to full merge (
(
Deniélou and Yoshida 2013
)

and Definition
3.6
in this paper) for projection
and its “dual” for the localiser (Definition
3.8
in this paper).

The
binary unmerge for localiser

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\sf unmL^{bin}}}
takes a type
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
(first argument) and “unmerges” it, according to the structure of two other types
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
(second and third arguments); thus
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\sf unmL^{bin}}}
returns a pair of types, the unmerged components of
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
. The non-binary version of the
unmerge for localiser

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\sf unmL}}
is defined by iterating the binary one, thus allowing for unmerging into
n
types. The role of this function is clarified by Lemma
B.11
.

To correctly define such function we need an auxiliary function that handles the case in which a projection merge is equal to a localiser one:
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\bigsqcup}_{i\in I}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\bigsqcup^{L}}_{j\in J}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}{}_{j}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
. In this case we want to obtain, for all
i\in I,j\in J
,
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}{}^{i}_{j}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
such that
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\bigsqcup}_{i}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}{}^{i}_{j}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}{}_{j}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\bigsqcup^{L}}_{j}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}{}^{i}_{j}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.

Definition B.8 (Localiser Unmerge for Projection Merge).

The partial function
binary localiser-unmerge for projection merge

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmLP^{bin}}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}
is recursively defined by the following equations, whenever the recursive call is defined (we use
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
to indicate standard pair projections):

\hskip 18.49988pt\text{where, for $n,m=1,2$,}\ \color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}{}^{n}_{m}}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\pi_{n}(\pi_{m}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmLP^{bin}}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}))\\[5.69054pt]
\text{undefined otherwise}\end{array}

The partial function
localiser-unmerge for projection merge

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmLP}\;L_{P}\;L_{L}}}
takes as input two lists of types instead of two pairs. It returns a tuple of lists: if
L_{P}=[\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\dots,\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}}{}_{n}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]
and
L_{L}=[\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\dots,\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}{}_{m}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]
the result list is of the kind
([\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}{}^{1}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\dots,\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}{}^{1}_{m}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}],\dots,[\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}{}^{n}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\dots,\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H}{}^{n}_{m}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}])
(
\pi_{i}
indicate the standard projection of the
i
-th component of the tuple).
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\sf unmLP}}
is defined by recursion (on the sum of the lengths of
L_{P}
and
L_{L}
) by the following equations.

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmLP}\;[]\;L_{L}}}=([])\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmLP}\;[\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]\;L_{L}}}=([L_{L}])\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmLP}\;[\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]\;L_{L}}}=\begin{cases}([],[])\ \text{if $L_{L}=[]$}\\
([\pi_{1}(\pi_{1}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmLP^{bin}}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}})),\pi_{1}(\pi_{2}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmLP^{bin}}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}))])\ \text{if $L_{L}=[\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]$}\\
([\pi_{1}(\pi_{1}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmLP^{bin}}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}})),\pi_{2}(\pi_{1}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmLP^{bin}}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}))],\\
\ \ [\pi_{1}(\pi_{2}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmLP^{bin}}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}})),\pi_{2}(\pi_{2}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmLP^{bin}}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}))])\ \text{if $L_{L}=[\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]$}\\
(\pi_{1}(\pi_{1}(U))\#L^{1},\pi_{1}(\pi_{2}(U))\#L^{2})\\
\hskip 18.49988pt\text{if $L_{L}=[\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\dots,\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}{}_{m}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]$, where, for $i=1,2$}\\
\hskip 18.49988ptL^{i}=\pi_{i}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmLP}\;[(\pi_{2}(\pi_{1}(U))),(\pi_{2}(\pi_{2}(U)))]\;[\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\dots,\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}{}_{m}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]}})\ \text{and}\\
\hskip 18.49988ptU={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmLP^{bin}}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\bigsqcup^{L}}_{k=2\dots m}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}{}_{k}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})}}\end{cases}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmLP}\;[\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\dots,\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}}{}_{n}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]\;L_{L}}}=(L^{1},\pi_{1}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmLP}\;[\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\dots,\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}}{}_{n}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]\;L^{2}}}),\dots,\pi_{n-1}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmLP}\;[\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\dots,\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}}{}_{n}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]\;L^{2}}}))\\
\hskip 18.49988pt\text{where, for $i=1,2$,}\ L^{i}={\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmLP}\;[\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},(\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\bigsqcup}_{k=2,\dots,n}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}}{}_{k}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})]\;L_{L}}}\end{array}

Lemma B.9.

We are given hybrid types
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\dots,\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}}{}_{n}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
and
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\dots,\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}{}_{m}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, such that
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\bigsqcup}_{i=1,\dots,n}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\bigsqcup^{L}}_{j=1,\dots,m}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}{}_{j}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
.

Then
\ {\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmLP}\;[\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},\dots,\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}}{}_{n}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}][\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\dots,\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}{}_{m}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]\
is defined and, if we set

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmLP}\;[\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}},\dots,\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}}{}_{n}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}][\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\dots,\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}{}_{m}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}]=([\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}{}^{1}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\dots,\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}{}^{1}_{m}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}],\dots,[\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}{}^{n}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\dots,\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}{}^{n}_{m}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}])\text{,}

we have that:

•

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\bigsqcup^{L}}_{j=1,\dots,m},\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}{}^{i}_{j}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{i}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, for all
i=1,\dots,n
, and

•

\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\bigsqcup}_{i=1,\dots,n},\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}{}^{i}_{j}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}=\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}}{}_{j}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, for all
j=1,\dots,n
.

Proof.

•

We first prove the base cases, in particular the case in which
n=2
and
m=2
(formally we first prove the result for
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\sf unmLP^{bin}}}
). This is done by structural induction on
\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}
, exploiting the hypothesis and the inductive nature both of
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\sqcup}
and
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\sqcup^{L}}
.

•

We conclude by induction on
n+m
, by following the definition of
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\sf unmLP}}
.

∎

We now define the unmerge for the localiser (Definition
B.10
) and ensure that it has the desired behaviour (Lemma
B.11
).

Definition B.10 (Localiser Unmerge).

The partial function
binary unmerge for localiser

{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmL^{bin}_{E}}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}
is defined if
{\textsf{ipart}}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\cup{\textsf{ipart}}({\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}})\subseteq E
by the following equations, by recursion on
\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\sf{depth}}(\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})
, whenever the recursive call is defined (we use
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
to indicate standard pair projections; we also make implicit the dependency on
E
using the notation
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\sf unmL^{bin}}}
instead of
ulbE
):

\begin{array}[]{l}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmL^{bin}}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}=(\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\mathtt{end}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmL^{bin}}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}=(\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{X}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmL^{bin}}\;(\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\mu\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{1}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{L}_{2}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}}=\begin{cases}(\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\mu\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.\pi_{1}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmL^{bin}}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{H^{L}_{1}}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{H^{L}_{2}}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}})}\color[rgb]{0,0,0},\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{\mu\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{X}\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}.\pi_{2}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{\sf unmL^{bin}}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{H^{\dagger}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{H^{L}_{1}}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\;\color[rgb]{0.1,0.4,0.1}{{H^{L}_{2}}^{\prime}}\color[rgb]{0,0,0}}})}\color[rgb]{0,0,0})\\
